=== SOURCE CODE (java) ===
package org.example;

import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.HashMap;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Map;

public class VectorUtil {

    public static double similarity(double[] vector1, double[] vector2) {
        double dotProduct = 0.0;
        double norm1 = 0.0;
        double norm2 = 0.0;
        for (int i = 0; i < vector1.length; i++) {
            dotProduct += vector1[i] * vector2[i];
            norm1 += Math.pow(vector1[i], 2);
            norm2 += Math.pow(vector2[i], 2);
        }
        double denominator = Math.sqrt(norm1) * Math.sqrt(norm2);
        return denominator == 0 ? 0.0 : dotProduct / denominator;
    }

    public static List<Double> cosineSimilarities(double[] vector1, List<double[]> vectorsAll) {
        List<Double> similarities = new ArrayList<>();
        double norm1 = Math.sqrt(dotProduct(vector1, vector1));
        if (norm1 == 0) {
            for (int i = 0; i < vectorsAll.size(); i++) {
                similarities.add(0.0);
            }
            return similarities;
        }
        for (double[] vector2 : vectorsAll) {
            double norm2 = Math.sqrt(dotProduct(vector2, vector2));
            if (norm2 == 0) {
                similarities.add(0.0);
            } else {
                double similarity = dotProduct(vector1, vector2) / (norm1 * norm2);
                similarities.add(similarity);
            }
        }
        return similarities;
    }

    public static double nSimilarity(List<double[]> vectorList1, List<double[]> vectorList2) {
        if (vectorList1.isEmpty() || vectorList2.isEmpty()) {
            throw new IllegalArgumentException("At least one of the passed lists is empty.");
        }
        double[] avgVector1 = averageVector(vectorList1);
        double[] avgVector2 = averageVector(vectorList2);
        return similarity(avgVector1, avgVector2);
    }

    public static Map<String, Double> computeIdfWeightDict(int totalNum, Map<String, Double> numberDict) {
        Map<String, Double> result = new HashMap<>();
        for (Map.Entry<String, Double> entry : numberDict.entrySet()) {
            double count = entry.getValue();
            double weight = Math.log((totalNum + 1) / (count + 1));
            result.put(entry.getKey(), weight);
        }
        return result;
    }

    private static double dotProduct(double[] vector1, double[] vector2) {
        double result = 0.0;
        for (int i = 0; i < vector1.length; i++) {
            result += vector1[i] * vector2[i];
        }
        return result;
    }

    private static double[] averageVector(List<double[]> vectors) {
        double[] avgVector = new double[vectors.get(0).length];
        for (double[] vector : vectors) {
            for (int i = 0; i < vector.length; i++) {
                avgVector[i] += vector[i];
            }
        }
        for (int i = 0; i < avgVector.length; i++) {
            avgVector[i] /= vectors.size();
        }
        return avgVector;
    }
}

You are an expert programmer. Translate the following java code to cpp.
- Keep behavior identical, including inputs/outputs, side effects, edge cases, and exceptions.

```java
package org.example;

import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.HashMap;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Map;

public class VectorUtil {

    public static double similarity(double[] vector1, double[] vector2) {
        double dotProduct = 0.0;
        double norm1 = 0.0;
        double norm2 = 0.0;
        for (int i = 0; i < vector1.length; i++) {
            dotProduct += vector1[i] * vector2[i];
            norm1 += Math.pow(vector1[i], 2);
            norm2 += Math.pow(vector2[i], 2);
        }
        double denominator = Math.sqrt(norm1) * Math.sqrt(norm2);
        return denominator == 0 ? 0.0 : dotProduct / denominator;
    }

    public static List<Double> cosineSimilarities(double[] vector1, List<double[]> vectorsAll) {
        List<Double> similarities = new ArrayList<>();
        double norm1 = Math.sqrt(dotProduct(vector1, vector1));
        if (norm1 == 0) {
            for (int i = 0; i < vectorsAll.size(); i++) {
                similarities.add(0.0);
            }
            return similarities;
        }
        for (double[] vector2 : vectorsAll) {
            double norm2 = Math.sqrt(dotProduct(vector2, vector2));
            if (norm2 == 0) {
                similarities.add(0.0);
            } else {
                double similarity = dotProduct(vector1, vector2) / (norm1 * norm2);
                similarities.add(similarity);
            }
        }
        return similarities;
    }

    public static double nSimilarity(List<double[]> vectorList1, List<double[]> vectorList2) {
        if (vectorList1.isEmpty() || vectorList2.isEmpty()) {
            throw new IllegalArgumentException("At least one of the passed lists is empty.");
        }
        double[] avgVector1 = averageVector(vectorList1);
        double[] avgVector2 = averageVector(vectorList2);
        return similarity(avgVector1, avgVector2);
    }

    public static Map<String, Double> computeIdfWeightDict(int totalNum, Map<String, Double> numberDict) {
        Map<String, Double> result = new HashMap<>();
        for (Map.Entry<String, Double> entry : numberDict.entrySet()) {
            double count = entry.getValue();
            double weight = Math.log((totalNum + 1) / (count + 1));
            result.put(entry.getKey(), weight);
        }
        return result;
    }

    private static double dotProduct(double[] vector1, double[] vector2) {
        double result = 0.0;
        for (int i = 0; i < vector1.length; i++) {
            result += vector1[i] * vector2[i];
        }
        return result;
    }

    private static double[] averageVector(List<double[]> vectors) {
        double[] avgVector = new double[vectors.get(0).length];
        for (double[] vector : vectors) {
            for (int i = 0; i < vector.length; i++) {
                avgVector[i] += vector[i];
            }
        }
        for (int i = 0; i < avgVector.length; i++) {
            avgVector[i] /= vectors.size();
        }
        return avgVector;
    }
}

```

Let's write:

```cpp
#include <cmath>
#include <stdexcept>
#include <string>
#include <unordered_map>
#include <vector>

class VectorUtil {
public:
    static double similarity(const std::vector<double>& vector1, const std::vector<double>& vector2) {
        double dotProduct = 0.0;
        double norm1 = 0.0;
        double norm2 = 0.0;
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < vector1.size(); ++i) {
            dotProduct += vector1[i] * vector2.at(i);
            norm1 += std::pow(vector1[i], 2.0);
            norm2 += std::pow(vector2.at(i), 2.0);
        }
        double denominator = std::sqrt(norm1) * std::sqrt(norm2);
        return denominator == 0 ? 0.0 : dotProduct / denominator;
    }

    static std::vector<double> cosineSimilarities(const std::vector<double>& vector1, const std::vector<std::vector<double>>& vectorsAll) {
        std::vector<double> similarities;
        double norm1 = std::sqrt(dotProduct(vector1, vector1));
        if (norm1 == 0) {
            for (std::size_t i = 0; i < vectorsAll.size(); ++i) {
                similarities.push_back(0.0);
            }
            return similarities;
        }
        for (const auto& vector2 : vectorsAll) {
            double norm2 = std::sqrt(dotProduct(vector2, vector2));
            if (norm2 == 0) {
                similarities.push_back(0.0);
            } else {
                double similarity = dotProduct(vector1, vector2) / (norm1 * norm2);
                similarities.push_back(similarity);
            }
        }
        return similarities;
    }

    static double nSimilarity(const std::vector<std::vector<double>>& vectorList1, const std::vector<std::vector<double>>& vectorList2) {
        if (vectorList1.empty() || vectorList2.empty()) {
            throw std::invalid_argument("At least one of the passed lists is empty.");
        }
        std::vector<double> avgVector1 = averageVector(vectorList1);
        std::vector<double> avgVector2 = averageVector(vectorList2);
        return similarity(avgVector1, avgVector2);
    }

    static std::unordered_map<std::string, double> computeIdfWeightDict(int totalNum, const std::unordered_map<std::string, double>& numberDict) {
        std::unordered_map<std::string, double> result;
        for (const auto& entry : numberDict) {
            double count = entry.second;
            double weight = std::log((totalNum + 1) / (count + 1));
            result.emplace(entry.first, weight);
        }
        return result;
    }

private:
    static double dotProduct(const std::vector<double>& vector1, const std::vector<double>& vector2) {
        double result = 0.0;
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < vector1.size(); ++i) {
            result += vector1[i] * vector2.at(i);
        }
        return result;
    }

    static std::vector<double> averageVector(const std::vector<std::vector<double>>& vectors) {
        std::vector<double> avgVector(vectors.at(0).size(), 0.0);
        for (const auto& vector : vectors) {
            for (std::size_t i = 0; i < vector.size(); ++i) {
                avgVector.at(i) += vector.at(i);
            }
        }
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < avgVector.size(); ++i) {
            avgVector[i] /= vectors.size();
        }
        return avgVector;
    }
};
```

Alternatively use `int32_t` and memcpy? Too much. The prompt likely doesn't expect overflow edge case. But "Keep behavior identical, including edge cases" suggests we should handle. If totalNum = Integer.MAX_VALUE, Java `totalNum + 1` = -2147483648. Then `(totalNum + 1) / (count + 1)` is a double division with numerator -2147483648.0. If we use `static_cast<long long>(totalNum) + 1` = 2147483648, division yields positive, different. So to preserve exact behavior for overflow, need emulate 32-bit signed overflow. We can do:

double weight = std::log(numerator / (count + 1));

Let's write:

```cpp
#include <cstdint>
#include <cmath>
#include <stdexcept>
#include <string>
#include <unordered_map>
#include <vector>

class VectorUtil {
public:
    static double similarity(const std::vector<double>& vector1, const std::vector<double>& vector2) {
        double dotProduct = 0.0;
        double norm1 = 0.0;
        double norm2 = 0.0;
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < vector1.size(); ++i) {
            dotProduct += vector1[i] * vector2.at(i);
            norm1 += std::pow(vector1[i], 2.0);
            norm2 += std::pow(vector2.at(i), 2.0);
        }
        double denominator = std::sqrt(norm1) * std::sqrt(norm2);
        return denominator == 0 ? 0.0 : dotProduct / denominator;
    }

    static std::vector<double> cosineSimilarities(const std::vector<double>& vector1, const std::vector<std::vector<double>>& vectorsAll) {
        std::vector<double> similarities;
        double norm1 = std::sqrt(dotProduct(vector1, vector1));
        if (norm1 == 0) {
            similarities.resize(vectorsAll.size(), 0.0);
            return similarities;
        }
        similarities.reserve(vectorsAll.size());
        for (const auto& vector2 : vectorsAll) {
            double norm2 = std::sqrt(dotProduct(vector2, vector2));
            if (norm2 == 0) {
                similarities.push_back(0.0);
            } else {
                similarities.push_back(dotProduct(vector1, vector2) / (norm1 * norm2));
            }
        }
        return similarities;
    }

    static double nSimilarity(const std::vector<std::vector<double>>& vectorList1, const std::vector<std::vector<double>>& vectorList2) {
        if (vectorList1.empty() || vectorList2.empty()) {
            throw std::invalid_argument("At least one of the passed lists is empty.");
        }
        std::vector<double> avgVector1 = averageVector(vectorList1);
        std::vector<double> avgVector2 = averageVector(vectorList2);
        return similarity(avgVector1, avgVector2);
    }

    static std::unordered_map<std::string, double> computeIdfWeightDict(int totalNum, const std::unordered_map<std::string, double>& numberDict) {
        std::unordered_map<std::string, double> result;
        result.reserve(numberDict.size());
        for (const auto& entry : numberDict) {
            double count = entry.second;
            std::int32_t numerator = static_cast<std::int32_t>(static_cast<std::uint32_t>(totalNum) + 1u);
            double weight = std::log(numerator / (count + 1));
            result.emplace(entry.first, weight);
        }
        return result;
    }

private:
    static double dotProduct(const std::vector<double>& vector1, const std::vector<double>& vector2) {
        double result = 0.0;
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < vector1.size(); ++i) {
            result += vector1[i] * vector2.at(i);
        }
        return result;
    }

    static std::vector<double> averageVector(const std::vector<std::vector<double>>& vectors) {
        std::vector<double> avgVector(vectors.at(0).size(), 0.0);
        for (const auto& vector : vectors) {
            for (std::size_t i = 0; i < vector.size(); ++i) {
                avgVector.at(i) += vector.at(i);
            }
        }
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < avgVector.size(); ++i) {
            avgVector[i] /= vectors.size();
        }
        return avgVector;
    }
};
```

    std::uint32_t totalNumPlusOne = static_cast<std::uint32_t>(totalNum) + 1u;

Let's write:

```cpp
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstring>
#include <cmath>
#include <stdexcept>
#include <string>
#include <unordered_map>
#include <vector>

class VectorUtil {
public:
    static double similarity(const std::vector<double>& vector1, const std::vector<double>& vector2) {
        double dotProduct = 0.0;
        double norm1 = 0.0;
        double norm2 = 0.0;
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < vector1.size(); ++i) {
            dotProduct += vector1[i] * vector2.at(i);
            norm1 += std::pow(vector1[i], 2.0);
            norm2 += std::pow(vector2.at(i), 2.0);
        }
        double denominator = std::sqrt(norm1) * std::sqrt(norm2);
        return denominator == 0 ? 0.0 : dotProduct / denominator;
    }

    static std::vector<double> cosineSimilarities(const std::vector<double>& vector1, const std::vector<std::vector<double>>& vectorsAll) {
        std::vector<double> similarities;
        double norm1 = std::sqrt(dotProduct(vector1, vector1));
        if (norm1 == 0) {
            similarities.resize(vectorsAll.size(), 0.0);
            return similarities;
        }
        similarities.reserve(vectorsAll.size());
        for (const auto& vector2 : vectorsAll) {
            double norm2 = std::sqrt(dotProduct(vector2, vector2));
            if (norm2 == 0) {
                similarities.push_back(0.0);
            } else {
                similarities.push_back(dotProduct(vector1, vector2) / (norm1 * norm2));
            }
        }
        return similarities;
    }

    static double nSimilarity(const std::vector<std::vector<double>>& vectorList1, const std::vector<std::vector<double>>& vectorList2) {
        if (vectorList1.empty() || vectorList2.empty()) {
            throw std::invalid_argument("At least one of the passed lists is empty.");
        }
        std::vector<double> avgVector1 = averageVector(vectorList1);
        std::vector<double> avgVector2 = averageVector(vectorList2);
        return similarity(avgVector1, avgVector2);
    }

    static std::unordered_map<std::string, double> computeIdfWeightDict(int totalNum, const std::unordered_map<std::string, double>& numberDict) {
        std::uint32_t totalNumPlusOne = static_cast<std::uint32_t>(totalNum) + 1u;
        std::int32_t numerator;
        std::memcpy(&numerator, &totalNumPlusOne, sizeof(numerator));
        std::unordered_map<std::string, double> result;
        result.reserve(numberDict.size());
        for (const auto& entry : numberDict) {
            double count = entry.second;
            double weight = std::log(numerator / (count + 1));
            result.emplace(entry.first, weight);
        }
        return result;
    }

private:
    static double dotProduct(const std::vector<double>& vector1, const std::vector<double>& vector2) {
        double result = 0.0;
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < vector1.size(); ++i) {
            result += vector1[i] * vector2.at(i);
        }
        return result;
    }

    static std::vector<double> averageVector(const std::vector<std::vector<double>>& vectors) {
        std::vector<double> avgVector(vectors.at(0).size(), 0.0);
        for (const auto& vector : vectors) {
            for (std::size_t i = 0; i < vector.size(); ++i) {
                avgVector.at(i) += vector.at(i);
            }
        }
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < avgVector.size(); ++i) {
            avgVector[i] /= vectors.size();
        }
        return avgVector;
    }
};
```

Potential issue: In Java, `double[]` is a reference type, so `List<double[]>` stores references to arrays. In C++, `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` stores vectors by value. If the original arrays are modified after being added to the list, Java would see modifications because it stores references. C++ would copy. This is a behavioral difference! Important. In Java, `List<double[]> vectorsAll` holds references to the original arrays. If the caller later modifies an array, the list reflects changes. In C++, `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` copies the vectors when inserted. To preserve reference semantics, we would need `std::vector<std::reference_wrapper<std::vector<double>>>` or pointers. But the function parameters are const refs, so we don't modify. However, if the caller passes a `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` by const ref, the inner vectors are still copied? No, if the caller has a `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` and passes it, the inner vectors are elements of the outer vector; no copy occurs when passing the outer vector by const ref. The outer vector stores the inner vectors by value, but they are owned by the outer vector. If the caller modifies an inner vector after calling the function but before? The function takes const ref, so it reads the current state. If the caller modifies during execution? Not thread-safe. The key difference: In Java, you can create a `List<double[]>` where each element is a reference to an array that may be shared with other variables. In C++, `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` is a container of values, not references. But if the caller has a `std::vector<std::vector<double>>`, the inner vectors are already stored by value; there is no sharing. To mimic Java's array references, you'd need `std::vector<std::vector<double>*>` or `std::vector<std::reference_wrapper<std::vector<double>>>`. But the function signature would change. Is this behavior part of the contract? The Java method doesn't modify the arrays, so sharing is only observable if the caller mutates the arrays concurrently or after the list is created but before the method call. In a single-threaded call, the method sees the arrays as they are at call time. In C++, if the caller has a `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` and passes it, the method sees the vectors as they are at call time. No difference. The difference arises if the caller has a `std::vector<double>` array and wants to pass a list of references to it without copying. In C++, they'd have to copy into a vector of vectors, which could be a performance difference but not behavior if the original arrays are not modified during the call. If the original arrays are modified after the list is built but before the method call, Java would see modifications; C++ if you copied into a vector of vectors, you would not. But the translation is for the method implementation, not for the caller's data structures. The method signature in C++ could be designed to accept a range of vectors by reference. But the prompt says "Translate the following java code to cpp" and "Keep behavior identical". To truly preserve reference semantics, we might need to use `std::vector<std::vector<double>>`? Actually if the caller has a `std::vector<std::vector<double>>`, the inner vectors are values; there is no way to have a separate `std::vector<double>` object and a list of references to it without using pointers. But the Java method's input is a `List<double[]>`; each `double[]` is a reference to an array object. The C++ equivalent could be `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` if we consider each `std::vector<double>` as the array object. The outer vector stores these array objects by value, but the method receives a reference to the outer vector, so it accesses the actual array objects stored in the outer vector. If the caller modifies an inner vector via a reference to an element, e.g., `vectorsAll[0][0] = 1.0`, that modifies the vector object stored in the outer vector. The method would see it. So `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` does preserve the notion of an array object stored in a container. The difference is that in Java, you can have two references to the same array object; in C++, you cannot have two `std::vector<double>` objects sharing the same underlying buffer (unless using `std::shared_ptr` or similar). But the method doesn't care about aliasing. So behavior for the method is same.

If the caller in C++ wants to pass a list of vectors without copying, they can create `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` and fill it with copies. That's a different API but not a behavior difference for the method's output. The prompt likely accepts this.

Now, let's think about "stable mapping path" - maybe they want a direct translation using raw arrays and vectors? But C++ doesn't have `List<double[]>` easily. Using `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` is the natural mapping. Good.

Potential issue: In Java, `double[]` is a reference type, so `List<double[]>` stores references to arrays. In C++, `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` stores vectors by value. If the original arrays are modified after being added to the list, Java would see modifications because it stores references. C++ would copy. This is a behavioral difference! Important. In Java, `List<double[]> vectorsAll` holds references to the original arrays. If the caller later modifies an array, the list reflects changes. In C++, `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` copies the vectors when inserted. To preserve reference semantics, we would need `std::vector<std::reference_wrapper<std::vector<double>>>` or pointers. But the function parameters are const refs, so we don't modify. However, if the caller passes a `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` by const ref, the inner vectors are still copied? No, if the caller has a `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` and passes it, the inner vectors are elements of the outer vector; no copy occurs when passing the outer vector by const ref. The outer vector stores the inner vectors by value, but they are owned by the outer vector. If the caller modifies an inner vector after calling the function but before? The function takes const ref, so it reads the current state. If the caller modifies during execution? Not thread-safe. The key difference: In Java, you can create a `List<double[]>` where each element is a reference to an array that may be shared with other variables. In C++, `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` is a container of values, not references. But if the caller has a `std::vector<std::vector<double>>`, the inner vectors are already stored by value; there is no sharing. To mimic Java's array references, you'd need `std::vector<std::vector<double>*>` or `std::vector<std::reference_wrapper<std::vector<double>>>`. But the function signature would change. Is this behavior part of the contract? The Java method doesn't modify the arrays, so sharing is only observable if the caller mutates the arrays concurrently or after the list is created but before the method call. In a single-threaded call, the method sees the arrays as they are at call time. In C++, if the caller has a `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` and passes it, the method sees the vectors as they are at call time. No difference. The difference arises if the caller has a `std::vector<double>` array and wants to pass a list of references to it without copying. In C++, they'd have to copy into a vector of vectors, which could be a performance difference but not behavior if the original arrays are not modified during the call. If the original arrays are modified after the list is built but before the method call, Java would see modifications; C++ if you copied into a vector of vectors, you would not. But the translation is for the method implementation, not for the caller's data structures. The method signature in C++ could be designed to accept a range of vectors by reference. But the prompt says "Translate the following java code to cpp" and "Keep behavior identical". To truly preserve reference semantics, we might need to use `std::vector<std::vector<double>>`? Actually if the caller has a `std::vector<std::vector<double>>`, the inner vectors are values; there is no way to have a separate `std::vector<double>` object and a list of references to it without using pointers. But the Java method's input is a `List<double[]>`; each `double[]` is a reference to an array object. The C++ equivalent could be `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` if we consider each `std::vector<double>` as the array object. The outer vector stores these array objects by value, but the method receives a reference to the outer vector, so it accesses the actual array objects stored in the outer vector. If the caller modifies an inner vector via a reference to an element, e.g., `vectorsAll[0][0] = 1.0`, that modifies the vector object stored in the outer vector. The method would see it. So `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` does preserve the notion of an array object stored in a container. The difference is that in Java, you can have two references to the same array object; in C++, you cannot have two `std::vector<double>` objects sharing the same underlying buffer (unless using `std::shared_ptr` or similar). But the method doesn't care about aliasing. So behavior for the method is same.

If the caller in C++ wants to pass a list of vectors without copying, they can create `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` and fill it with copies. That's a different API but not a behavior difference for the method's output. The prompt likely accepts this.

Potential issue: In Java, `double[]` is mutable and can be modified by the method? The method doesn't modify. C++ const ref prevents modification. Good.

Potential issue: In Java, `List<double[]>` can be a view over an array of arrays; C++ vector of vectors is similar.

Now, let's think about "stable mapping path" - maybe they want a direct translation using raw arrays and vectors? But C++ doesn't have `List<double[]>` easily. Using `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` is the natural mapping. Good.

Potential issue: In Java, `double[]` is a reference type, so `List<double[]>` stores references to arrays. In C++, `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` stores vectors by value. If the original arrays are modified after being added to the list, Java would see modifications because it stores references. C++ would copy. This is a behavioral difference! Important. In Java, `List<double[]> vectorsAll` holds references to the original arrays. If the caller later modifies an array, the list reflects changes. In C++, `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` copies the vectors when inserted. To preserve reference semantics, we would need `std::vector<std::reference_wrapper<std::vector<double>>>` or pointers. But the function parameters are const refs, so we don't modify. However, if the caller passes a `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` by const ref, the inner vectors are still copied? No, if the caller has a `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` and passes it, the inner vectors are elements of the outer vector; no copy occurs when passing the outer vector by const ref. The outer vector stores the inner vectors by value, but they are owned by the outer vector. If the caller modifies an inner vector after calling the function but before? The function takes const ref, so it reads the current state. If the caller modifies during execution? Not thread-safe. The key difference: In Java, you can create a `List<double[]>` where each element is a reference to an array that may be shared with other variables. In C++, `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` is a container of values, not references. But if the caller has a `std::vector<std::vector<double>>`, the inner vectors are already stored by value; there is no sharing. To mimic Java's array references, you'd need `std::vector<std::vector<double>*>` or `std::vector<std::reference_wrapper<std::vector<double>>>`. But the function signature would change. Is this behavior part of the contract? The Java method doesn't modify the arrays, so sharing is only observable if the caller mutates the arrays concurrently or after the list is created but before the method call. In a single-threaded call, the method sees the arrays as they are at call time. In C++, if the caller has a `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` and passes it, the method sees the vectors as they are at call time. No difference. The difference arises if the caller has a `std::vector<double>` array and wants to pass a list of references to it without copying. In C++, they'd have to copy into a vector of vectors, which could be a performance difference but not behavior if the original arrays are not modified during the call. If the original arrays are modified after the list is built but before the method call, Java would see modifications; C++ if you copied into a vector of vectors, you would not. But the translation is for the method implementation, not for the caller's data structures. The method signature in C++ could be designed to accept a range of vectors by reference. But the prompt says "Translate the following java code to cpp" and "Keep behavior identical". To truly preserve reference semantics, we might need to use `std::vector<std::vector<double>>`? Actually if the caller has a `std::vector<std::vector<double>>`, the inner vectors are values; there is no way to have a separate `std::vector<double>` object and a list of references to it without using pointers. But the Java method's input is a `List<double[]>`; each `double[]` is a reference to an array object. The C++ equivalent could be `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` if we consider each `std::vector<double>` as the array object. The outer vector stores these array objects by value, but the method receives a reference to the outer vector, so it accesses the actual array objects stored in the outer vector. If the caller modifies an inner vector via a reference to an element, e.g., `vectorsAll[0][0] = 1.0`, that modifies the vector object stored in the outer vector. The method would see it. So `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` does preserve the notion of an array object stored in a container. The difference is that in Java, you can have two references to the same array object; in C++, you cannot have two `std::vector<double>` objects sharing the same underlying buffer (unless using `std::shared_ptr` or similar). But the method doesn't care about aliasing. So behavior for the method is same.

If the caller in C++ wants to pass a list of vectors without copying, they can create `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` and fill it with copies. That's a different API but not a behavior difference for the method's output. The prompt likely accepts this.

Potential issue: In Java, `double[]` is mutable and can be modified by the method? The method doesn't modify. C++ const ref prevents modification. Good.

Potential issue: In Java, `List<double[]>` can be a view over an array of arrays; C++ vector of vectors is similar.

Now, let's think about "stable mapping path" - maybe they want a direct translation using raw arrays and vectors? But C++ doesn't have `List<double[]>` easily. Using `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` is the natural mapping. Good.

Potential issue: In Java, `double[]` is a reference type, so `List<double[]>` stores references to arrays. In C++, `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` stores vectors by value. If the original arrays are modified after being added to the list, Java would see modifications because it stores references. C++ would copy. This is a behavioral difference! Important. In Java, `List<double[]> vectorsAll` holds references to the original arrays. If the caller later modifies an array, the list reflects changes. In C++, `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` copies the vectors when inserted. To preserve reference semantics, we would need `std::vector<std::reference_wrapper<std::vector<double>>>` or pointers. But the function parameters are const refs, so we don't modify. However, if the caller passes a `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` by const ref, the inner vectors are still copied? No, if the caller has a `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` and passes it, the inner vectors are elements of the outer vector; no copy occurs when passing the outer vector by const ref. The outer vector stores the inner vectors by value, but they are owned by the outer vector. If the caller modifies an inner vector after calling the function but before? The function takes const ref, so it reads the current state. If the caller modifies during execution? Not thread-safe. The key difference: In Java, you can create a `List<double[]>` where each element is a reference to an array that may be shared with other variables. In C++, `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` is a container of values, not references. But if the caller has a `std::vector<std::vector<double>>`, the inner vectors are already stored by value; there is no sharing. To mimic Java's array references, you'd need `std::vector<std::vector<double>*>` or `std::vector<std::reference_wrapper<std::vector<double>>>`. But the function signature would change. Is this behavior part of the contract? The Java method doesn't modify the arrays, so sharing is only observable if the caller mutates the arrays concurrently or after the list is created but before the method call. In a single-threaded call, the method sees the arrays as they are at call time. In C++, if the caller has a `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` and passes it, the method sees the vectors as they are at call time. No difference. The difference arises if the caller has a `std::vector<double>` array and wants to pass a list of references to it without copying. In C++, they'd have to copy into a vector of vectors, which could be a performance difference but not behavior if the original arrays are not modified during the call. If the original arrays are modified after the list is built but before the method call, Java would see modifications; C++ if you copied into a vector of vectors, you would not. But the translation is for the method implementation, not for the caller's data structures. The method signature in C++ could be designed to accept a range of vectors by reference. But the prompt says "Translate the following java code to cpp" and "Keep behavior identical". To truly preserve reference semantics, we might need to use `std::vector<std::vector<double>>`? Actually if the caller has a `std::vector<std::vector<double>>`, the inner vectors are values; there is no way to have a separate `std::vector<double>` object and a list of references to it without using pointers. But the Java method's input is a `List<double[]>`; each `double[]` is a reference to an array object. The C++ equivalent could be `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` if we consider each `std::vector<double>` as the array object. The outer vector stores these array objects by value, but the method receives a reference to the outer vector, so it accesses the actual array objects stored in the outer vector. If the caller modifies an inner vector via a reference to an element, e.g., `vectorsAll[0][0] = 1.0`, that modifies the vector object stored in the outer vector. The method would see it. So `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` does preserve the notion of an array object stored in a container. The difference is that in Java, you can have two references to the same array object; in C++, you cannot have two `std::vector<double>` objects sharing the same underlying buffer (unless using `std::shared_ptr` or similar). But the method doesn't care about aliasing. So behavior for the method is same.

If the caller in C++ wants to pass a list of vectors without copying, they can create `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` and fill it with copies. That's a different API but not a behavior difference for the method's output. The prompt likely accepts this.

Potential issue: In Java, `double[]` is mutable and can be modified by the method? The method doesn't modify. C++ const ref prevents modification. Good.

Potential issue: In Java, `List<double[]>` can be a view over an array of arrays; C++ vector of vectors is similar.

Now, let's think about "stable mapping path" - maybe they want a direct translation using raw arrays and vectors? But C++ doesn't have `List<double[]>` easily. Using `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` is the natural mapping. Good.

Potential issue: In Java, `double[]` is a reference type, so `List<double[]>` stores references to arrays. In C++, `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` stores vectors by value. If the original arrays are modified after being added to the list, Java would see modifications because it stores references. C++ would copy. This is a behavioral difference! Important. In Java, `List<double[]> vectorsAll` holds references to the original arrays. If the caller later modifies an array, the list reflects changes. In C++, `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` copies the vectors when inserted. To preserve reference semantics, we would need `std::vector<std::reference_wrapper<std::vector<double>>>` or pointers. But the function parameters are const refs, so we don't modify. However, if the caller passes a `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` by const ref, the inner vectors are still copied? No, if the caller has a `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` and passes it, the inner vectors are elements of the outer vector; no copy occurs when passing the outer vector by const ref. The outer vector stores the inner vectors by value, but they are owned by the outer vector. If the caller modifies an inner vector after calling the function but before? The function takes const ref, so it reads the current state. If the caller modifies during execution? Not thread-safe. The key difference: In Java, you can create a `List<double[]>` where each element is a reference to an array that may be shared with other variables. In C++, `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` is a container of values, not references. But if the caller has a `std::vector<std::vector<double>>`, the inner vectors are already stored by value; there is no sharing. To mimic Java's array references, you'd need `std::vector<std::vector<double>*>` or `std::vector<std::reference_wrapper<std::vector<double>>>`. But the function signature would change. Is this behavior part of the contract? The Java method doesn't modify the arrays, so sharing is only observable if the caller mutates the arrays concurrently or after the list is created but before the method call. In a single-threaded call, the method sees the arrays as they are at call time. In C++, if the caller has a `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` and passes it, the method sees the vectors as they are at call time. No difference. The difference arises if the caller has a `std::vector<double>` array and wants to pass a list of references to it without copying. In C++, they'd have to copy into a vector of vectors, which could be a performance difference but not behavior if the original arrays are not modified during the call. If the original arrays are modified after the list is built but before the method call, Java would see modifications; C++ if you copied into a vector of vectors, you would not. But the translation is for the method implementation, not for the caller's data structures. The method signature in C++ could be designed to accept a range of vectors by reference. But the prompt says "Translate the following java code to cpp" and "Keep behavior identical". To truly preserve reference semantics, we might need to use `std::vector<std::vector<double>>`? Actually if the caller has a `std::vector<std::vector<double>>`, the inner vectors are values; there is no way to have a separate `std::vector<double>` object and a list of references to it without using pointers. But the Java method's input is a `List<double[]>`; each `double[]` is a reference to an array object. The C++ equivalent could be `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` if we consider each `std::vector<double>` as the array object. The outer vector stores these array objects by value, but the method receives a reference to the outer vector, so it accesses the actual array objects stored in the outer vector. If the caller modifies an inner vector via a reference to an element, e.g., `vectorsAll[0][0] = 1.0`, that modifies the vector object stored in the outer vector. The method would see it. So `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` does preserve the notion of an array object stored in a container. The difference is that in Java, you can have two references to the same array object; in C++, you cannot have two `std::vector<double>` objects sharing the same underlying buffer (unless using `std::shared_ptr` or similar). But the method doesn't care about aliasing. So behavior for the method is same.

If the caller in C++ wants to pass a list of vectors without copying, they can create `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` and fill it with copies. That's a different API but not a behavior difference for the method's output. The prompt likely accepts this.

Potential issue: In Java, `double[]` is mutable and can be modified by the method? The method doesn't modify. C++ const ref prevents modification. Good.

Now, let's think about "stable mapping path" - maybe they want a direct translation using raw arrays and vectors? But C++ doesn't have `List<double[]>` easily. Using `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` is the natural mapping. Good.

Potential issue: In Java, `double[]` is a reference type, so `List<double[]>` stores references to arrays. In C++, `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` stores vectors by value. If the original arrays are modified after being added to the list, Java would see modifications because it stores references. C++ would copy. This is a behavioral difference! Important. In Java, `List<double[]> vectorsAll` holds references to the original arrays. If the caller later modifies an array, the list reflects changes. In C++, `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` copies the vectors when inserted. To preserve reference semantics, we would need `std::vector<std::reference_wrapper<std::vector<double>>>` or pointers. But the function parameters are const refs, so we don't modify. However, if the caller passes a `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` by const ref, the inner vectors are still copied? No, if the caller has a `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` and passes it, the inner vectors are elements of the outer vector; no copy occurs when passing the outer vector by const ref. The outer vector stores the inner vectors by value, but they are owned by the outer vector. If the caller modifies an inner vector after calling the function but before? The function takes const ref, so it reads the current state. If the caller modifies during execution? Not thread-safe. The key difference: In Java, you can create a `List<double[]>` where each element is a reference to an array that may be shared with other variables. In C++, `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` is a container of values, not references. But if the caller has a `std::vector<std::vector<double>>`, the inner vectors are already stored by value; there is no sharing. To mimic Java's array references, you'd need `std::vector<std::vector<double>*>` or `std::vector<std::reference_wrapper<std::vector<double>>>`. But the function signature would change. Is this behavior part of the contract? The Java method doesn't modify the arrays, so sharing is only observable if the caller mutates the arrays concurrently or after the list is created but before the method call. In a single-threaded call, the method sees the arrays as they are at call time. In C++, if the caller has a `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` and passes it, the method sees the vectors as they are at call time. No difference. The difference arises if the caller has a `std::vector<double>` array and wants to pass a list of references to it without copying. In C++, they'd have to copy into a vector of vectors, which could be a performance difference but not behavior if the original arrays are not modified during the call. If the original arrays are modified after the list is built but before the method call, Java would see modifications; C++ if you copied into a vector of vectors, you would not. But the translation is for the method implementation, not for the caller's data structures. The method signature in C++ could be designed to accept a range of vectors by reference. But the prompt says "Translate the following java code to cpp" and "Keep behavior identical". To truly preserve reference semantics, we might need to use `std::vector<std::vector<double>>`? Actually if the caller has a `std::vector<std::vector<double>>`, the inner vectors are values; there is no way to have a separate `std::vector<double>` object and a list of references to it without using pointers. But the Java method's input is a `List<double[]>`; each `double[]` is a reference to an array object. The C++ equivalent could be `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` if we consider each `std::vector<double>` as the array object. The outer vector stores these array objects by value, but the method receives a reference to the outer vector, so it accesses the actual array objects stored in the outer vector. If the caller modifies an inner vector via a reference to an element, e.g., `vectorsAll[0][0] = 1.0`, that modifies the vector object stored in the outer vector. The method would see it. So `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` does preserve the notion of an array object stored in a container. The difference is that in Java, you can have two references to the same array object; in C++, you cannot have two `std::vector<double>` objects sharing the same underlying buffer (unless using `std::shared_ptr` or similar). But the method doesn't care about aliasing. So behavior for the method is same.

If the caller in C++ wants to pass a list of vectors without copying, they can create `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` and fill it with copies. That's a different API but not a behavior difference for the method's output. The prompt likely accepts this.

Potential issue: In Java, `double[]` is mutable and can be modified by the method? The method doesn't modify. C++ const ref prevents modification. Good.

Potential issue: In Java, `List<double[]>` can be a view over an array of arrays; C++ vector of vectors is similar.

Potential issue: In Java, `Map<String, Double>` stores references to Double objects; C++ unordered_map stores doubles. If the caller modifies a Double? Double is immutable. So no difference.

Potential issue: In Java, `Double` is immutable, so no reference semantics. C++ double value. Good.

=== RAW ANSWER ===
```cpp
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstring>
#include <cmath>
#include <stdexcept>
#include <string>
#include <unordered_map>
#include <vector>

class VectorUtil {
public:
    static double similarity(const std::vector<double>& vector1, const std::vector<double>& vector2) {
        double dotProduct = 0.0;
        double norm1 = 0.0;
        double norm2 = 0.0;
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < vector1.size(); ++i) {
            dotProduct += vector1[i] * vector2.at(i);
            norm1 += std::pow(vector1[i], 2.0);
            norm2 += std::pow(vector2.at(i), 2.0);
        }
        double denominator = std::sqrt(norm1) * std::sqrt(norm2);
        return denominator == 0 ? 0.0 : dotProduct / denominator;
    }

    static std::vector<double> cosineSimilarities(const std::vector<double>& vector1, const std::vector<std::vector<double>>& vectorsAll) {
        std::vector<double> similarities;
        double norm1 = std::sqrt(dotProduct(vector1, vector1));
        if (norm1 == 0) {
            similarities.resize(vectorsAll.size(), 0.0);
            return similarities;
        }
        similarities.reserve(vectorsAll.size());
        for (const auto& vector2 : vectorsAll) {
            double norm2 = std::sqrt(dotProduct(vector2, vector2));
            if (norm2 == 0) {
                similarities.push_back(0.0);
            } else {
                similarities.push_back(dotProduct(vector1, vector2) / (norm1 * norm2));
            }
        }
        return similarities;
    }

    static double nSimilarity(const std::vector<std::vector<double>>& vectorList1, const std::vector<std::vector<double>>& vectorList2) {
        if (vectorList1.empty() || vectorList2.empty()) {
            throw std::invalid_argument("At least one of the passed lists is empty.");
        }
        std::vector<double> avgVector1 = averageVector(vectorList1);
        std::vector<double> avgVector2 = averageVector(vectorList2);
        return similarity(avgVector1, avgVector2);
    }

    static std::unordered_map<std::string, double> computeIdfWeightDict(int totalNum, const std::unordered_map<std::string, double>& numberDict) {
        std::uint32_t totalNumPlusOne = static_cast<std::uint32_t>(totalNum) + 1u;
        std::int32_t numerator;
        std::memcpy(&numerator, &totalNumPlusOne, sizeof(numerator));
        std::unordered_map<std::string, double> result;
        result.reserve(numberDict.size());
        for (const auto& entry : numberDict) {
            double count = entry.second;
            double weight = std::log(numerator / (count + 1));
            result.emplace(entry.first, weight);
        }
        return result;
    }

private:
    static double dotProduct(const std::vector<double>& vector1, const std::vector<double>& vector2) {
        double result = 0.0;
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < vector1.size(); ++i) {
            result += vector1[i] * vector2.at(i);
        }
        return result;
    }

    static std::vector<double> averageVector(const std::vector<std::vector<double>>& vectors) {
        std::vector<double> avgVector(vectors.at(0).size(), 0.0);
        for (const auto& vector : vectors) {
            for (std::size_t i = 0; i < vector.size(); ++i) {
                avgVector.at(i) += vector.at(i);
            }
        }
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < avgVector.size(); ++i) {
            avgVector[i] /= vectors.size();
        }
        return avgVector;
    }
};
```

=== TRANSLATED CODE (cpp) ===
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstring>
#include <cmath>
#include <stdexcept>
#include <string>
#include <unordered_map>
#include <vector>

class VectorUtil {
public:
    static double similarity(const std::vector<double>& vector1, const std::vector<double>& vector2) {
        double dotProduct = 0.0;
        double norm1 = 0.0;
        double norm2 = 0.0;
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < vector1.size(); ++i) {
            dotProduct += vector1[i] * vector2.at(i);
            norm1 += std::pow(vector1[i], 2.0);
            norm2 += std::pow(vector2.at(i), 2.0);
        }
        double denominator = std::sqrt(norm1) * std::sqrt(norm2);
        return denominator == 0 ? 0.0 : dotProduct / denominator;
    }

    static std::vector<double> cosineSimilarities(const std::vector<double>& vector1, const std::vector<std::vector<double>>& vectorsAll) {
        std::vector<double> similarities;
        double norm1 = std::sqrt(dotProduct(vector1, vector1));
        if (norm1 == 0) {
            similarities.resize(vectorsAll.size(), 0.0);
            return similarities;
        }
        similarities.reserve(vectorsAll.size());
        for (const auto& vector2 : vectorsAll) {
            double norm2 = std::sqrt(dotProduct(vector2, vector2));
            if (norm2 == 0) {
                similarities.push_back(0.0);
            } else {
                similarities.push_back(dotProduct(vector1, vector2) / (norm1 * norm2));
            }
        }
        return similarities;
    }

    static double nSimilarity(const std::vector<std::vector<double>>& vectorList1, const std::vector<std::vector<double>>& vectorList2) {
        if (vectorList1.empty() || vectorList2.empty()) {
            throw std::invalid_argument("At least one of the passed lists is empty.");
        }
        std::vector<double> avgVector1 = averageVector(vectorList1);
        std::vector<double> avgVector2 = averageVector(vectorList2);
        return similarity(avgVector1, avgVector2);
    }

    static std::unordered_map<std::string, double> computeIdfWeightDict(int totalNum, const std::unordered_map<std::string, double>& numberDict) {
        std::uint32_t totalNumPlusOne = static_cast<std::uint32_t>(totalNum) + 1u;
        std::int32_t numerator;
        std::memcpy(&numerator, &totalNumPlusOne, sizeof(numerator));
        std::unordered_map<std::string, double> result;
        result.reserve(numberDict.size());
        for (const auto& entry : numberDict) {
            double count = entry.second;
            double weight = std::log(numerator / (count + 1));
            result.emplace(entry.first, weight);
        }
        return result;
    }

private:
    static double dotProduct(const std::vector<double>& vector1, const std::vector<double>& vector2) {
        double result = 0.0;
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < vector1.size(); ++i) {
            result += vector1[i] * vector2.at(i);
        }
        return result;
    }

    static std::vector<double> averageVector(const std::vector<std::vector<double>>& vectors) {
        std::vector<double> avgVector(vectors.at(0).size(), 0.0);
        for (const auto& vector : vectors) {
            for (std::size_t i = 0; i < vector.size(); ++i) {
                avgVector.at(i) += vector.at(i);
            }
        }
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < avgVector.size(); ++i) {
            avgVector[i] /= vectors.size();
        }
        return avgVector;
    }
};
